# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Activity 1: Expanding access to climate finance for developing countries

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 1.1: Enhancing access to climate finance**
  - **Objectives:** Facilitate access to climate finance for developing countries through the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other international funds.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Increased number of accredited entities from developing countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for potential applicants.

## Activity 2: Enhancing climate resilience

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 2.1: Improving climate information services**
  - **Objectives:** Strengthen the provision and use of climate information services to enhance climate resilience in vulnerable communities.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved climate information services for at least five countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local meteorological services.

- **Activity 2.2: Enhancing early warning systems**
  - **Objectives:** Improve the effectiveness of early warning systems and disaster risk management in vulnerable communities.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Strengthened early warning systems in at least three countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local disaster management authorities.

## Activity 3: Implementing climate-smart agriculture

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 3.1: Promoting climate-smart agriculture**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of agricultural systems to climate change through the adoption of climate-smart practices.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Adoption of climate-smart practices by at least five thousand farmers.

- **Activity 3.2: Improving water management**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance water management practices in agriculture to improve productivity and resilience to climate change.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved water management systems in at least three countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local agricultural authorities.

## Activity 4: Implementing renewable energy projects

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 4.1: Promoting renewable energy**
  - **Objectives:** Facilitate the adoption of renewable energy technologies by developing countries.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Installation of at least five thousand renewable energy systems in rural communities.

- **Activity 4.2: Improving access to clean energy**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance access to affordable and reliable clean energy for vulnerable communities.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved access to clean energy for at least three million people in developing countries.

## Activity 5: Implementing climate-smart cities

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 5.1: Promoting climate-smart urban planning**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of urban areas through climate-smart planning and design.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Implementation of climate-smart urban plans in at least five cities.

- **Activity 5.2: Improving urban infrastructure**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of urban infrastructure to climate change.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved urban infrastructure in at least three cities.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local urban planning authorities.

## Activity 6: Implementing climate-smart health

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 6.1: Promoting climate-smart health practices**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of health systems to climate change through the adoption of climate-smart practices.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Adoption of climate-smart health practices by at least five thousand health facilities.

- **Activity 6.2: Improving health infrastructure**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of health infrastructure to climate change.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved health infrastructure in at least three countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local health authorities.

## Activity 7: Implementing climate-smart education

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 7.1: Promoting climate-smart education**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of educational systems to climate change through the adoption of climate-smart practices.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Adoption of climate-smart education practices by at least five thousand schools.

- **Activity 7.2: Improving educational infrastructure**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of educational infrastructure to climate change.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved educational infrastructure in at least three countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local educational authorities.

## Activity 8: Implementing climate-smart communication

### Sub-activities
- **Activity 8.1: Promoting climate-smart communication**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of communication systems to climate change through the adoption of climate-smart practices.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Adoption of climate-smart communication practices by at least five thousand communication facilities.

- **Activity 8.2: Improving communication infrastructure**
  - **Objectives:** Enhance the resilience of communication infrastructure to climate change.
  - **Deliverables:**
    - Improved communication infrastructure in at least three countries.
    - Enhanced capacity building programs for local communication authorities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Activities

### Activity 1.1 Capacity building for farmers to support climate-smart agriculture

### Activity 1.2 Knowledge sharing and learning among actors in the agricultural value chain

## Objectives

- **Objective 1.1**: Farmers to better understand and adopt climate-smart practices.
- **Objective 1.2**: Knowledge exchange among actors in the agricultural value chain.

## Interventions

- **Intervention 1.1.1**: Training on climate-smart agriculture for farmers.
    - *Description*: Conduct training sessions on climate-smart farming techniques, crop diversification, and sustainable land management practices.
    - *Target Group*: Farmers.
    - *Timeline*: October to December.

- **Intervention 1.1.2**: Establishment of farmer field schools.
    - *Description*: Set up demonstration plots where farmers can learn and practice new techniques.
    - *Target Group*: Farmers.
    - *Timeline*: January to March.

- **Intervention 1.2.1**: Knowledge exchange workshops.
    - *Description*: Organize workshops for knowledge sharing among farmers, extension workers, and agricultural experts.
    - *Target Group*: Farmers, extension workers, agricultural experts.
    - *Timeline*: April to June.

## Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

- **Frequency**: Quarterly reports, approximately 12 reports per year.
- **Responsibility**: The Project Manager will oversee the preparation of quarterly progress reports. These reports will be reviewed by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) for approval before submission to the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

### Oversight and monitoring requirements

- **Oversight**: The Project Manager will ensure that all project activities are implemented effectively.
- **Monitoring**: Regular monitoring and supervision visits will be conducted by the Project Manager.

### 187.
The NMDP County Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through regular submittals of progress reports to the NMDP County Office. The NMDP County Office is responsible for compliance with UNDP project and UNDP financial management policies and procedures, and will ensure that all required documentation is submitted in a timely manner.

### 188.
A project kickoff workshop will be held after the UNDP project launch to signify the start of the project. This meeting will include an overview of the project plan, including objectives, timelines, and responsibilities. The workshop will also provide an opportunity for the Project Manager to meet with key stakeholders and present the project plan.

### 189.
The Project Manager, UNDP County Office, and the NMDP Project Technical Advisor will provide technical assistance to the Annual Progress Report (APR) to each project implementation. The Project Manager will ensure that the APR is submitted on time and in accordance with UNDP guidelines. The Project Manager will also ensure that the APR is reviewed by the NMDP County Office.

### 190.
No independent end-term review process will be undertaken, but the findings and recommendations contained in the project implementation evaluation (IE) will be used to inform future projects implemented by the NMDP County Office. The IE will be conducted by an external consultant hired by UNDP Project Technical Advisor.

### 191.
Evaluation findings
- The IE will take place no later than three months prior to operational closure at the end of the project implementation period, or as otherwise agreed with the UNDP Country Office.

### 192.
The NMDP County Office will also provide the government with an evaluation report in line with UNDP Country Office guidelines. The IE will be conducted by an external consultant hired by the NMDP County Office and will be funded by the UNDP Project Technical Advisor.

### 193.
The NMDP County Office will retain all M&E records for this project for up to ten years after project finance closure, in line with UNDP project documentation guidelines. The IE report will be submitted to the UNDP Country Office and the government.

# G. GC POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G1. Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (50 words, approximately 1.3 pages)

Environmental and social risk assessment is a critical component of project preparation. It helps identify potential adverse environmental and social impacts that may arise from the implementation of a policy or project. This process enables the identification of mitigation measures to avoid or minimize adverse impacts. Approved and relevant environmental and social policies and standards are applied to ensure that projects are managed in an environmentally and socially sustainable manner. The objective is to achieve no net loss of biodiversity, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and enhance community well-being. The World Bank Group's Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) provides a comprehensive approach to manage environmental and social risks associated with projects. It aims to ensure that projects are designed and implemented in a way that avoids or minimizes adverse environmental and social impacts.

## G2. Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (50 words, approximately 1 page)

Gender equality is a fundamental human right and a key element of the World Bank Group's twin goals of ending extreme poverty and promoting shared prosperity. The World Bank Group is committed to achieving gender equality and women's empowerment in all its operations. This includes ensuring that projects are designed and implemented in a way that promotes gender equality and empowers women and girls.

## G3. Climate Change (50 words, approximately 1 page)

Climate change affects the agricultural sector and the country, climate change and to ensure food security and nutrition, it is necessary to implement measures to mitigate and adapt to climate change. The World Bank Group is committed to supporting countries in their efforts to address the challenges posed by climate change.

## G4. Disaster Risk Management (50 words, approximately 1 page)

Disaster risk management is a critical component of development planning. The World Bank Group supports countries in their efforts to manage disaster risks and build resilience. This includes providing technical assistance and financing for projects that aim to reduce the impact of disasters on communities.

## G5. Governance and Anti-Corruption (50 words, approximately 1 page)

Good governance is essential for sustainable development. The World Bank Group supports countries in their efforts to promote good governance and combat corruption. This includes providing technical assistance and financing for projects that aim to strengthen institutions and promote transparency and accountability.

## G6. Financial Management (50 words, approximately 1 page)

Effective financial management is critical for the successful implementation of development projects. The World Bank Group supports countries in their efforts to improve financial management practices. This includes providing technical assistance and financing for projects that aim to strengthen public financial management systems.

The NM Guidelines are a formal part of UNCDP policies and procedures, as set out in the UNCDP Programme and Operational Policies (POPP). The NM Guidelines were developed and included by reference to POPP. The NM Guidelines will be reviewed periodically and updated as necessary.

The standard examining entity is MOFAC, also referred to as its national Implementation Partner of UNCDP. The examining entity will include the NM Guidelines in its examination process. Both the examining entity and the National Implementation Partner are governed by the Bend.

These processes are designed to ensure that cash transfers are implemented in a transparent, accountable, and efficient manner.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP170

## Proposal name:
Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water resource management and sustainable agriculture practices

## Accepted entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

## Country(ies):
Thailand

## Project/programme size:
Small

## L. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
The project contributes to global efforts of climate change mitigation and adaptation by enhancing agricultural productivity through improved water management, flood risk reduction, and more efficient use of land. Thailand is a major exporter of rice and other agricultural products, so the project’s impact on food security could be significant.

Thailand has made considerable progress in its efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change. The country has implemented several measures to achieve these goals, including the development of a National Climate Change Master Plan and the adoption of renewable energy targets.

The project builds on existing national policies and strategies for water resource management and sustainable agriculture, which are aligned with the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other international initiatives. This alignment can help ensure that the project’s activities are consistent with broader efforts to address climate change.

### Points of concern
There is a lack of detail on how the project will be implemented, including specific activities, timelines, and indicators for success. The project’s objectives are broad and could benefit from more specific targets and milestones to ensure accountability and transparency.

The project does not provide sufficient details on how it will engage with local communities and stakeholders, including farmers, water user associations, and other relevant actors. This is important to ensure that the project is responsive to local needs and priorities and that it builds on existing efforts and capacities at the national and sub-national levels.

The project’s objectives are not fully aligned with the strategic priorities of the GCF and other international initiatives, which could limit its potential for scaling up and replication. The project does not provide sufficient details on how it will contribute to broader efforts to address climate change and support sustainable development.

There is a lack of detail on how the project will be monitored and evaluated, including specific indicators and methodologies for tracking progress and measuring impact. This is important to ensure that the project is delivering results and that lessons learned can be used to improve future initiatives.

# Green Climate Fund

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### I. Project description

The project demonstrates an innovative approach to climate change adaptation and mitigation by addressing the needs of vulnerable communities in the Caribbean region. The project aims to support the implementation of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) through the promotion of climate-resilient infrastructure, renewable energy, and improved water management practices.

The project will be implemented in three countries: Belize, Guyana, and Saint Lucia. The project activities include:

- Construction of climate-resilient infrastructure
- Implementation of renewable energy projects
- Improvement of water management systems

These activities aim to enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change by reducing their exposure to natural hazards and improving their adaptive capacity.

### Points of discussion

There is need to ensure strong community engagement in the planning, implementation, and monitoring of project activities. It is important that local knowledge and traditional ecological knowledge are integrated into the project design. The engaged risk management approach is commendable as it ensures that communities are prepared for potential climate-related disasters. The project’s alignment with national priorities and international commitments is well-documented.

The proposed risk management strategy should be strengthened to ensure comprehensive coverage of all potential risks. The project’s long-term sustainability will depend on the effectiveness of its monitoring and evaluation system, which needs to be robustly designed and implemented.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background

The project represents a critical step in addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation for vulnerable communities in the Caribbean region. The project aims to support the implementation of NDCs by promoting climate-resilient infrastructure, renewable energy, and improved water management practices.

The project will be implemented in three countries: Belize, Guyana, and Saint Lucia. The activities include:

- Construction of climate-resilient infrastructure
- Implementation of renewable energy projects
- Improvement of water management systems

These activities aim to enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change by reducing their exposure to natural hazards and improving their adaptive capacity.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the views and recommendations of the Secretariat and the Executive Committee, respectively.

# Increased drought risk

The frequency of extreme SPEI has increased, supporting an increase in drought.

## Drought impact on agricultural yield

Historical data shows that a 10% decrease in rainfall results in a 10% decrease in crop yields. This is expected to continue with the increasing frequency and intensity of droughts.

### Significant increases (percentiles 50PI) in frequency from 2070 to 2100

The Climate Prediction Center Rainfall Atlas is used for these projections. In Thailand, it is projected that rainfall will decrease by 30% in the northern region and increase by 40% in the southern region. This change will affect agricultural productivity and crop yields.

### The Chao Phraya River Basin

The Chao Phraya River Basin is one of the most vital river systems in Thailand. It supports over 25 million people, providing water for agriculture and industrial use. The basin also supports a diverse ecosystem with over 10,000 species of plants and animals. The river system is crucial for the economy, providing water for irrigation, supporting fisheries, and agricultural activities.

### Projected impacts

- **Water availability**: Reduced rainfall will lead to decreased water availability, impacting agriculture and industrial use.
- **Crop yields**: Decreased rainfall will reduce crop yields, affecting food security.
- **Ecosystem services**: Reduced water flow will impact ecosystem services, such as flood control and irrigation.

### Adaptation measures

Adaptation measures include improving water management practices, implementing efficient irrigation systems, and promoting sustainable land use. These measures can help mitigate the impacts of drought on agriculture and ecosystems.

## Components by component analysis

### Component 1: Baseline scenario (without adaptation)

The baseline scenario assumes no additional adaptation beyond current practices. This scenario is used to establish a reference point for evaluating the impact of climate change on water resources.

### Component 2: Adaptation measures

Adaptation measures include improving water management practices, implementing efficient irrigation systems, and promoting sustainable land use. These measures can help mitigate the impacts of drought on agriculture and ecosystems.

### Component 3: Cost-benefit analysis

A cost-benefit analysis is conducted to evaluate the economic feasibility of adaptation measures. This includes assessing the costs and benefits of different adaptation strategies.

### Component 4: Sensitivity analysis

Sensitivity analysis is performed to assess the impact of uncertainty in key parameters, such as rainfall projections and water demand. This helps identify potential risks and uncertainties in the adaptation strategy.

# This output will also enhance inter-institutional collaboration in water management, enabling stronger collaboration between key decision-making bodies in Thailand’s water sector and improving the integration of water resources management (WRM) with other sectors.

Chapter 2. Integrated water management through strengthened industrial cooperation (as per the 10th EDF)

This chapter focuses on a comprehensive EIF framework to improve water management in the industrial sector. The framework will be designed to develop new strategies, update the system of indicators, and provide guidance for the development of an integrated water resources management plan. This will help to enhance the efficiency of water use by industries.

In the context of Thailand’s water management, Thailands’ largely dependent on grey infrastructure, although there are examples where of nature-based solutions have been established. GCF financing can support the integration of nature-based solutions into existing water management systems. While comprehensive best practices exist across all sectors, this chapter focuses on the industrial sector. The integration of nature-based solutions in a net-zero carbon future will require a greater understanding of the opportunities and challenges associated with the integration of nature-based solutions in water management. This is especially important for industries that have significant environmental impacts.

Project: River Basin. The project is designed to promote the use of integrated water resources management (IWRM) approaches, including the development of an integrated river basin management plan. This chapter will provide practical training on flood management decision-making processes for water resources managers. This will help to enhance the capacity of water resources managers to make informed decisions about water resources management.

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Title

Agroecology and sustainable management of agricultural landscapes to increase productivity and climate resilience in smallholder farming systems in Thailand

### Project Description

This project combines with optimum irrigation efficiency. This is coupled with an enhanced soil moisture retention capacity (HORTI box), which provides more distributed water application, thereby reducing the need for irrigation during dry seasons. The project also aims to improve water management.

Thailand has developed a climate change mitigation and adaptation plan for the agricultural sector. The project will be implemented in the northern region of Thailand by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the Department of Agriculture. The project will focus on improving the livelihoods of smallholder farmers through agroecological practices to increase their resilience to climate change.

The project aims to improve the livelihoods of 10,000 smallholder farmers in the northern region of Thailand by implementing sustainable agricultural practices. The project will also aim to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture by 50% by 2030.

### Sustainable Development Potential

- The project contributes to Sustainable Development Goals (1) No Poverty, (2) Zero Hunger, (3) Good Health and Well-being, (4) Quality Education, (5) Gender Equality, (6) Clean Water and Sanitation, (7) Affordable and Clean Energy, (8) Decent Work and Economic Growth, (9) Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure, (10) Reduced Inequalities, (11) Sustainable Cities and Communities, (12) Responsible Consumption and Production, (13) Climate Action, (14) Life Below Water, (15) Life on Land.

The GBC will assess environmental and social risks and opportunities using the Environmental Standards Procedure (ESSP) and an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The assessment process includes:

The GBC understands that activities will respect the core labour standards set by the International Labour Organization and the principles contained in the UN Global Compact. The GBC will:

### Introduction

The authorized entity (AE) has provided a gender equality and gender action plan (GAP).

### AE's Commitment

The AE has provided a greater commitment based on a clear review and understanding of the current context. This includes the development of a comprehensive approach to addressing gender issues, including the provision of training and support for employees.

### Gender Equality Plan

The AE has developed a Gender Equality Plan (GEP) that focuses on the following areas:

- Providing equal opportunities for all employees
- Ensuring fair treatment and respect for all employees
- Promoting diversity and inclusion in the workplace
- Addressing any barriers to equality and promoting a culture of inclusivity

### Implementation

The AE has implemented a number of initiatives to promote gender equality, including:

- Providing training on unconscious bias and inclusive language
- Establishing mentoring programs for women
- Creating employee resource groups for LGBTQ+ employees
- Developing policies to support flexible working arrangements
- Conducting regular surveys to assess employee satisfaction with the AE's gender equality efforts

### Results

The AE has seen positive results from its gender equality initiatives, including:

- Increased representation of women in leadership positions
- Improved employee engagement and retention rates
- Enhanced reputation as an employer of choice for diverse talent

### Future Plans

The AE is committed to continuing its efforts to promote gender equality and plans to:

- Expand its mentoring programs to include more employees
- Develop additional policies to support work-life balance
- Continue to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness of its gender equality initiatives

# Green Climate Fund

## c) Appeal from their roots in agriculture, women are also linked closely with the additional tasks of cleaning, cooking, childcare, etc. (more than 5 hours a day on domestic tasks). This is exacerbated by climate change impacts such as droughts and floods which reduce water availability for home consumption.

Water for home consumption is also the responsibility of women, including those who bear in mind that fetching water is a time-consuming task. Women are often the first to suffer from water scarcity and are more vulnerable to their effects by fetching water or facing the same challenges. When the burden falls on them, it can lead to women having less time with family members, less time for rest and recreation, and less time for income-generating activities.

The additional burdens of fetching water means that women have less time to engage in other productive activities such as farming, power over water resources is crucial, particularly poor and rural women who are already vulnerable. Women's participation in decision-making processes related to water management is essential to ensure that their needs and priorities are addressed. This includes ensuring that women have access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities.

This will also help to identify the most vulnerable groups and communities, and to develop appropriate interventions.

# Legal assessment

a. The project has been approved by the accredited entity on 5 August 2018 and becomes effective on 23 November 2016.

b. The AI has provided a certificate confirming that he obtained an internal approval from the relevant authority in Thailand to implement the project.

c. The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Thailand, a country in which the Green Climate Fund (GCF) does not provide direct access to funding. The GCF does not provide grants for foreign entities to engage in activities outside their own countries. Therefore, the AI has obtained an accreditation agreement with the Government of Thailand on 13 October 2016. However, so that the project can be implemented by the AI, it is recommended that the AI signs a sub-agreement with the accredited entity.

d. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both approved for it to legally proceed to address their respective activities and/or concerns in relation to this project. Furthermore, it is recommended that submissions to GCF are made only after the AI has obtained a sub-agreement with the accredited entity.

# List of proposed conditions (including legal)

a. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that my approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

b. Signature of the Standard activity agreement by a duly authorized signatory for the accredited entity and the AI.

c. Compliance of the project activities with the safeguards policies set out in the Standard.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP170

## Proposed title
Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water management and sustainable agriculture

### 1.2 Adaptation impact
Thailand’s vulnerability to climate change renders it a priority country for adaptation measures. The proposed project aims to enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers and rural communities in the lower Mekong Basin (LMB) through integrated water resource management (IWRM). This approach integrates year-round water availability, flood control, and drought mitigation into agricultural production systems. The project will also promote climate-smart agriculture (CSA), which combines improved crop varieties with sustainable water management practices to enhance productivity and resilience.

The proposed project seeks to enhance the water sector’s climate adaptation capacity by integrating CSA into IWRM. # Component 1

Component 1 is focused on improving the availability and quality of climate information and its applications to support decision-making processes in climate-sensitive sectors. This component will enhance the integration between the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) and other relevant institutions such as the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MONRE), the Department of Meteorology, Hydrology and Climate Change (DMHCC), and the Vietnam Institute for Disaster Management (VIDEM). It will also strengthen cooperation with regional and international organizations to enhance the capacity of NMHSs in providing climate services. The component will support the development of a comprehensive strategy for climate information services and the establishment of a national climate information system.

Although activity 1.1 includes the procurement and installation of climate and water monitoring equipment, it is not intended as a standalone project. Upon the approval of the independent evaluation report, the proposed activities should be integrated into the overall implementation plan of the component. The detailed budget for the overall submission was updated to include a list of all relevant expenditures, including the costs associated with the procurement of climate and water monitoring equipment. The budget also includes a contingency fund to address any unforeseen needs that may arise during the implementation.

Through implementation, this project would strengthen user engagement through field-based activities and training programs. It will enhance the capacity of NMHSs in providing climate information services and support the development of a comprehensive strategy for climate information services. The component will also support the establishment of a national climate information system to improve the availability and quality of climate information.

EBM measures are proposed to ensure compliance with the handbook on climate change adaptation, with specific objectives further prepared based on monitoring and evaluation indicators. The project will also provide support for the implementation of integrated risk management (such as the use of management systems) in climate-sensitive sectors. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is currently preparing a comprehensive guide to the preparation and submission of EBM measures that will be made available to all NMHSs.

The project will also support the development of a comprehensive strategy for climate information services, including the establishment of a national climate information system.

# Information regarding the time required for complete rehabilitation of the capital market

Information regarding the time required for complete rehabilitation of the capital market is important to investors and regulators alike. The approach to rehabilitation differs substantially across countries, but in all cases, it is a complex process that requires a combination of regulatory and supervisory actions. These actions include the adoption of new accounting standards, the implementation of new corporate governance practices, and the development of new regulatory frameworks. In addition, the rehabilitation process may also involve the restructuring of financial institutions and the provision of assistance to investors.

The rehabilitation process is a complex and time-consuming one that requires the involvement of many different stakeholders. These include the government, the central bank, the securities regulator, the stock exchange, and other market participants. The rehabilitation process may also involve the use of new technologies and tools, such as the use of artificial intelligence to detect fraudulent activity.

# Green Climate Fund

## I. The project’s support to climate resilient agriculture and its expanding access to markets

The project’s support to climate resilient agriculture and its expanding access to markets is a key component of the GCF’s strategy to promote sustainable development and poverty reduction for farming peoples.

### I.A. Gender equity and social inclusion

The project will ensure that women and men have equal opportunities in their access to resources, services, and decision-making processes. The project will also support the development of communication tools, social events, people with severe time to engage their communities in climate change adaptation and mitigation.

## II. Gender-equity and social inclusion report

### II.A. Needs of the recipient

#### II.A.1. Vulnerability of the country and climate resilience

The project design takes into account the vulnerability of the country and its climate resilience. The project will be implemented with the largest possible impact on the most vulnerable communities, with the greatest benefit to the poorest and most marginalized groups. The project will also support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy.

#### II.A.2. Climate resilient practices

The climate resilient practices proposed in this project are based on a participatory approach that involves local communities and their traditional knowledge. The project will promote the adoption of climate resilient practices, such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and integrated pest management.

#### II.A.3. Model ensembles

The validation of the model ensemble used for climate projections is made against observed data from the region. The models are also validated against observations from other regions with similar climatic conditions. The project will use a combination of global climate models (GCMs) and regional climate models (RCMs) to provide a comprehensive assessment of future climate change impacts.

### II.B. Additional information

Additional information on the project’s gender equity and social inclusion objectives can be found in the [Green Climate Fund Gender Policy](https://www.greenclimate.fund/gender-policy).

# Greenpeace Report

## Introduction

Therefore, the use of CHIRP radar datasets for the validation of gridded data and the use of these datasets to validate the results of the chemical transport model are presented here.

### Study Considerations

The study considered that while precipitation has become less frequent in some parts of the world, it is more intense. This is due to the increase in extreme weather events such as heavy rainfall. The study also considered the impact of climate change on the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The results show that the study area is mostly covered by a semi-arid climate with low precipitation levels. However, the study area has experienced an increase in extreme weather events over the past decade.

### Data Collection

Data were collected from various sources to validate the gridded data. These include:

- **TRMM (Tropical Rainfall Measuring Mission)**: Provides global rainfall estimates.
- **CHIRPS (Climate Hazards Group Infrared Precipitation with Station Data)**: Provides long-term precipitation estimates.
- **GPM (Global Precipitation Measurement)**: Provides global precipitation data.

Data were collected from these sources to validate the gridded data and to assess the impact of climate change on precipitation patterns.

### Results

The results show that:

1. The study area has experienced an increase in extreme weather events over the past decade.
2. The frequency of heavy rain has increased significantly, particularly during the monsoon season.
3. The study area is mostly covered by a semi-arid climate with low precipitation levels.
4. The use of CHIRP radar datasets for the validation of gridded data and the use of these datasets to validate the results of the chemical transport model are presented here.

### Conclusion

The study concludes that while precipitation has become less frequent in some parts of the world, it is more intense due to climate change. This is due to the increase in extreme weather events such as heavy rainfall. The study also considered the impact of climate change on the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The results show that the study area is mostly covered by a semi-arid climate with low precipitation levels. However, the study area has experienced an increase in extreme weather events over the past decade.

### Recommendations

The study recommends that:

1. Further research should be conducted to better understand the impact of climate change on precipitation patterns.
2. The use of CHIRP radar datasets for the validation of gridded data and the use of these datasets to validate the results of the chemical transport model should be continued.
3. The study area should continue to monitor the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

## References

- **TRMM (Tropical Rainfall Measuring Mission)**: Provides global rainfall estimates.
- **CHIRPS (Climate Hazards Group Infrared Precipitation with Station Data)**: Provides long-term precipitation estimates.
- **GPM (Global Precipitation Measurement)**: Provides global precipitation data.

# c. Urbanization and agricultural drought

Urbanization is a common phenomenon suffered regions across the globe, agricultural drought in a region is a major concern for the urban planners and policy makers. The impact of urbanization on the environment is well documented by the IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change). The urbanization process has led to an increase in the demand for water resources which in turn has resulted in an increase in the use of groundwater during the non-monsoon period when surface water is not available. This has led to a significant increase in the use of groundwater for irrigation and other purposes. The impact of this increase in the use of groundwater is well documented by the IPCC.

The impact of urbanization on the environment is well documented by the IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change). The urbanization process has led to an increase in the demand for water resources which in turn has resulted in an increase in the use of groundwater during the non-monsoon period when surface water is not available. This has led to a significant increase in the use of groundwater for irrigation and other purposes. The impact of this increase in the use of groundwater is well documented by the IPCC.

# d. Climate Change

Climate change (also known as global warming) refers to the changes in the statistical distribution of weather over prolonged periods of time, typically decades or longer. Climate change may refer to a change in average weather conditions, or in the time variation of weather within seasons and the range of extremes such as coldest temperatures or heaviest one-day snowfall.

Although almost 20 Climate Change (Climate Change) conclusions that "Trends in precipitation are not uniform across regions or over time." The IPCC's report concludes that there is no significant evidence to suggest that climate change has caused an increase in the frequency or intensity of tropical cyclones. The IPCC's report also states that it is likely that the number of tropical cyclones will decrease in the future.

# i. Vulnerable regions

The most vulnerable regions are those where droughts and flooding are the greatest threats to human health and food security, which are the group most vulnerable to climate change. The depth of the impact of climate change on agriculture is determined by a number of factors including the type of crop grown, the level of irrigation used, and the availability of water resources.

# ii. Economic and social development

The economic and social impacts of droughts can be significant, with some regions experiencing up to 10 per cent of their gross domestic product (GDP) lost due to drought. The impact of drought on agriculture is particularly severe in developing countries where agricultural systems are highly dependent on rainfall. In these countries, the impact of drought can be devastating, leading to food shortages and famine.

# iii. Alternative sources of financing

In 2015, studies have shown that upper-middle-income economies receive well over $1 billion per year in climate-related finance from multilateral development banks (MDBs) and other international public sector institutions. The majority of this funding is provided by the World Bank Group (WBG), which provides around $700 million per year to support climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts.

# iv. Absence of alternative sources of financing

The absence of alternative sources of financing can be a major barrier to the implementation of drought risk management strategies. In many cases, the lack of funding can lead to the underfunding of projects and programs that are designed to mitigate the impacts of drought.

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Title: Enhancing Climate Resilience of Agriculture and Livelihoods in Kenya through Improved Agricultural Technologies and Practices

### Introduction
The project aims to enhance climate resilience by building capacity, reducing risks, and improving livelihoods of school communities through the development of a 10-year strategic plan. The project will be implemented over five years with an initial focus on the first three years.

### Objectives
- **Objective 1:** Enhance the climate resilience of agriculture and livestock systems.
- **Objective 2:** Improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers through improved agricultural technologies and practices.
- **Objective 3:** Strengthen the capacity of local institutions to support climate-smart agriculture.

### Project Components
- **Component 1:** Development of climate-smart agriculture technologies.
- **Component 2:** Capacity building for climate-smart agriculture.
- **Component 3:** Institutional strengthening and policy development.

### Implementation Strategy
The project will be implemented through the following approaches:
1. Training in climate-smart agriculture.
2. Development and dissemination of improved agricultural technologies.
3. Strengthening local institutions to support climate-smart agriculture.

### Project Budget
The total budget for the project is USD 5,000,000 over five years.

### Risk Management
- **Risk of evasion or poaching:** The risk of evasion or poaching of TMD and RHB and other species will be managed through regular monitoring and capacity building.
- **Inadequate EAF resources:** Inadequate EAF resources may limit the project's ability to achieve its objectives. This risk will be mitigated by securing additional funding.

### Monitoring and Evaluation
The project will utilize standard indicators for monitoring progress, including:
1. Number of farmers trained in climate-smart agriculture.
2. Number of improved technologies adopted.
3. Increase in agricultural productivity.

### Conclusion
The project aims to enhance the resilience of agriculture and livelihoods in Kenya through improved agricultural practices and technologies, supported by strong institutional capacity.

# Gender Assessment, Action Plan and Budget

## Enhancing climate resilience in Thailand through effective water management and sustainable agriculture

### Introduction

This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Thailand, with a specific focus on improving climate resilience and environmental funding in water management and sustainable agriculture. The assessment will be used to inform the development of a gender-responsive action plan for enhancing opportunities at the policy and project level. The assessment is based on available data, literature review, and consultations.

- Understanding the impact of climate change on women and men
- Drawing on sector-specific considerations (e.g., employment benefits of agriculture)
- Integrating gender considerations in water management and sustainable agriculture
- Documenting on Thailand's relevant gender and climate commitments, including the SDGs
- Identifying key stakeholders for future engagement and action
- Integrating gender considerations in the project decisions, targets, and activities

### Gender and Climate Change Impacts in Thailand

Thailand is considered one of the countries most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The country's vulnerability is exacerbated by its reliance on agriculture, which is highly dependent on natural resources such as water and land. Climate change affects agricultural productivity and food security, particularly for smallholder farmers who rely on rain-fed farming systems.

Women are disproportionately affected by climate change due to their roles in the agricultural sector and their responsibilities within households. They face increased risks from natural disasters, which can disrupt livelihoods and increase poverty levels. Women also play a crucial role in managing household resources and ensuring food security for their families.

The impacts of climate change affect the agricultural sector and the country's climate resilience. Women are at greater risk due to their roles in agriculture and their responsibilities within households. They face increased risks from natural disasters, which can disrupt livelihoods and increase poverty levels. Women also play a crucial role in managing household resources and ensuring food security for their families.

### Table 1: Gender and Climate Change Impacts in Thailand

| Impact Area                | Women's Impact                          | Men's Impact                         |
|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| Agricultural Productivity    | Decreased agricultural productivity      | Decreased agricultural productivity   |
| Food Security              | Increased food insecurity               | Increased food insecurity            |
| Livelihoods                 | Disruption of livelihoods               | Disruption of livelihoods             |
| Household Resource Management | Increased responsibility for resource management | Increased responsibility for resource management |

### Conclusion

The gender assessment highlights the need for a comprehensive approach to addressing climate change impacts in Thailand. It emphasizes the importance of integrating gender considerations into water management and sustainable agriculture policies and practices.

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

Women as well as men are also both important change agents and critical actors in actions to address climate change. They bring different knowledge and skills, as well as needs and priorities, which are important for the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of climate change and natural resources projects.

### Ending Gender Inequality in Thailand

The gender inequality index (GII) measures gender inequality across three important dimensions of human development: reproductive health, measured by adolescent fertility rate and adolescent birth rate; empowerment, measured by proportion of parliamentary seats occupied by women and proportion of female graduates; and economic participation, measured as labor market participation rate of women. The GII ranges from 0 to 1, where 0 means that men and women are equal.

The 2017 GII ranks Thailand at the position of **93** out of **160** countries, which implies a gender gap in these areas of human development. The component indicators highlight areas in need of critical action to address gender inequality, including maternal health, empowerment, and economic participation.

Thailand has a GII value of 0.384, ranking **93** out of **160** countries in the 2017 index (see Table 1). This is an improvement from the previous year's rank of **95**. The sub-indexes show that Thailand has made progress in reproductive health and empowerment but lags behind in economic participation. For example, the adolescent birth rate for women aged 15-19 years is 47.8 births per 1,000 women (compared to 62.3 for men). In contrast, the proportion of parliamentary seats held by women is **19.2%** compared to **7.3%** for men. The labor force participation rate of women aged 15-24 years is **38.6%**, while that of men in the same age group is **53.0%**.

Source: UNDP Human Development Report 2019

In the OAI analysis, factors contributing to poverty of farming households include geographical remoteness, lack of access to markets, poor infrastructure, and limited access to credit. The project aims to reduce poverty with the priority to the poorest and contribute to the Green Plan. Roughly 60% of the population live on less than $1 a day, and 84% live below the national poverty line. The project will benefit approximately 35,000 people in rural areas.

Focus group discussions were held in the project sites during project development, including 4R1 including a strong emphasis. The focus of the discussion was on gender issues such as women's participation in decision-making processes and their involvement in the project. The results indicate that women are more likely to be involved in the long-term benefits over the life of the project. The focus group discussions also highlighted the importance of involving women in the planning, implementation, and monitoring of the project.

The next step is to ensure that gender issues are addressed during the design phase of the project. This includes ensuring that women have equal access to resources and opportunities for participation in decision-making processes.

### Gender Division of Labor

Women grow more vegetables than men do. This is in addition to the role of caring for families and household work. This is a consistent trend with studies in northern Thailand. There was found that women often hold the primary responsibility for household food consumption. Another study by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) found that women are involved in all aspects of agricultural production, which then dominate mechanical tasks. Further, women are significantly related to ensuring food, including households, seeds, breed and plant varieties, and livestock. Women also play a significant role in marketing activities.

Water insecurity creates household-related challenges and potential losses of income. A shortage of water for irrigation is a major concern among farmers. In northern Thailand, often work support their mothers with household labor. They also contribute to the household, particularly for women and children as primary household caretakers. "Household gross division in the project sites indicates that for managing domestic activities, women are more involved than men." This includes cooking, cleaning, washing, and other household chores.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Funding Project

### Context

Communities have noted a 2019 national survey which indicated that in rural areas, women spend an average of 5.4 hours per day on unpaid care work compared to 1.6 hours for men.

### Access to Resources

#### The Land's Constitution (Articles 13 and 43) and the Civil and Commercial Code (Section 1386)

Women have equal rights to inheritance as men. The law allows joint ownership and co-ownership of property for married and unmarried couples. Women and their families equally benefit from inheritance if there is no will. However, women's influence in decision-making in rural areas, communities, with communal land, affecting resource management and conservation, is limited.

#### Civil and Commercial Code

The Civil and Commercial Code stipulates that after divorce, commonly held property is divided between the spouses. The law does not recognize individual property like joint well (Thamlad, 1975, Sec. 1478) is considered common property, such as the spouse's personal management or one spouse's contribution to the marriage.

### Women and Financial Services

Women are more likely to own bank accounts, urban wealth, and access formal financial services. Government measures such as the National Village and Urban Community Fund extend loans to women for small businesses and agricultural purposes.

### Access to Credit

#### Microfinance Institutions (MFIs)

Microfinance institutions provide credit to rural areas, particularly women. The government has supported MFIs through the National Housing and Mortgage Project, which provides low-interest loans to women for housing and small business development.

#### Formal Financial Services

Women have access to formal financial services such as commercial banks and microfinance institutions. However, cultural norms and lack of collateral limit their ability to obtain credit.

### Legal Framework

#### Land Rights Act B.E. 2543 (2000)

The Land Rights Act B.E. 2543 (2000) allows women equal rights to land ownership. The law also provides for joint ownership and co-ownership of property for married couples.

### Recommendations

1. **Promote Women's Participation**: Encourage women's participation in decision-making processes related to resource management.
2. **Improve Access to Credit**: Enhance access to credit by providing more flexible loan terms and reducing collateral requirements.
3. **Strengthen Legal Framework**: Strengthen the legal framework to ensure equal rights for women in property ownership and inheritance.

### References

- Thamlad, 1975
- National Housing and Mortgage Project

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Social norms, inequitable power dynamics, and the gendered division of labor affect women’s and men’s access to and control over resources, including land, water, and decision-making power. Women are not always part of the planning and implementation of interventions that aim to improve their lives. Women’s roles in agriculture and natural resource management are often overlooked or undervalued.

A recent study demonstrates some of the challenges faced by the communities that benefit from the programs funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF). First, poor governance and lack of transparency can undermine the effectiveness of interventions. Second, gender inequalities persist. In 10 percent of the GCF projects reviewed, participants mentioned that new project funds had exacerbated existing inequalities between women and men.

Thatcher’s 1997 Education Act grants “all children, without discrimination,” a right to a quality education and the Ministry of Education supports all children’s right to free primary education. However, gender disparities persist in access to education. In 2018, the government school enrollment rate for girls in remote areas was 65 percent compared with 73 percent for boys. In urban areas, the enrollment rates were higher: 94 percent for girls and 97 percent for boys. The gender gap has decreased considerably from slightly more than half (51%) in 2001 to nearly two-thirds (66%) in 2018.

There was also a difference between urban and rural areas. In 2018, the enrollment rate of girls in urban areas was higher than that of boys by 3 percentage points. In contrast, in rural areas, the enrollment rate of boys was higher than that of girls by 4 percentage points.

The government has made efforts to improve access to education for all, and many of GCF activities are ongoing in Thailand. Yet, girls continue to face challenges accessing education including: lower literacy rates, longer travel times, and additional responsibilities at home. Marginalized populations also face barriers to accessing education.

From the perspective of higher education, women have more opportunities than men; however, although 53 percent of science students are female, only 13 percent of female students pursue higher education as a female. However, the number of female students in higher education has increased over time.

For example, in 2018, the government launched a campaign to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment. The campaign aims to eliminate discrimination against women and girls and to promote their full and equal participation in society. The campaign also seeks to raise awareness about the importance of gender equality and to encourage men and boys to support women’s rights.

### Results

Thailand has worked to ensure equal access to health services including small and reproductive health clinic services. In primary schools, are systems required to use districts regarding the 3080, and across to health care becomes more equitable. From a gender perspective, achievements include the following:

- **Health insurance coverage**: 97% of the population is covered by universal health care.
- **Maternal mortality rate**: 46 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2015.
- **Infant mortality rate**: 18.3 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2015.
- **Child mortality rate**: 9.7 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2015.

Further, the maternal mortality ratio is 46 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2015. The child mortality rate is 9.7 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2015. The total fertility rate is 1.5 children per woman in the 1970s to 1.6 in 2011.

Challenges continue however, including the increase of adolescent birth rate from 31 per 1,000 women aged 15-19 years in 2000 to 48 in 2013, an increase for contraception use among sexually active adolescents aged 15-19 years (from 6.9% in 2000 to 13.4% in 2013), and the prevalence of HIV/AIDS among young people aged 15-24 years (18.3%).

The government is working on improving health promotion and disease prevention, including the use of a national code of ethics for medical professionals. The government has also established a national code of ethics for medical professionals.

Indigenous peoples in rural Thailand experience health inequalities an demonstrated by a recent study to be disproportionately affected by HIV/AIDS. Indigenous women have limited access to public health services, leading to negative health outcomes. Many women with disabilities are unable to access health care and face discrimination. Many women with disabilities also lack access to reproductive health services.

This page presents 13 key findings from the Gender Assessment Thailand Report, page 5, 2018.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund

The impacts of COVID-19 in Thailand and in the world are not yet fully known. The project beneficiaries include vulnerable households, many female-headed and elderly households, who are a high-risk category.

### Existing legislation

Article 8 of the Constitution states that men and women of Thailand have the same rights to hold public and political office in the equality, justice, and dignity principles (Thailand, 2017). There is no legal provision for gender-based violence. However, there are provisions for protection against domestic violence, including training for family petition attorneys, public education campaigns, and support services for victims of domestic violence. The Gender Equality Act provides for equal opportunities in political participation; for example, women's seats in local government bodies, and the establishment of a committee to address gender issues. Elective offices, hiring, promotion, and other employment-related decisions must be made without discrimination based on sex.

Women are still underrepresented in public and private sectors, including in the parliament, government, and military at all administrative levels. Thailand has been rated as one of the lowest countries in terms of women's participation in political decision-making processes. The World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report 2018 ranked Thailand 95th out of 149 countries. Women's representation in parliament is only 16%, and their representation in local government bodies is even lower at 7%.

### Women's health

Women's health is a major concern in Thailand, especially among the poor. The maternal mortality rate is high compared to other countries in Southeast Asia. The infant mortality rate is also high. The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates that one woman dies every two hours from complications of pregnancy and childbirth.

### Gender-based violence

Gender-based violence is a serious problem in Thailand, especially against women in the public and private spheres. Thailand does not currently have laws addressing all forms of violence against women. However, there are some provisions for protection against domestic violence under the Civil and Commercial Code (Act II). Law B.E. 2539 (1996) provides for criminal penalties for rape, sexual harassment, and other forms of gender-based violence. The law also provides for protection orders and compensation for victims.

caused harm to the body, mind or health of a family member; and to exert coercion or sexual interference on women and children in order to wrongfully obtain money from them (Acts 360 to 362, 375 to 381).

Rape is criminalized by the Penal Code of 1965, and the subsequent Common Code Amendment Act of 1998. The Labor Protection Act of 2008 and the Penal Code forbid night protection in the workplace and the Labor Protection Act extends protection against sexual demands from employers to female employees. The law also criminalizes sexual harassment (courtesy to be on view) in the workplace, and women may be verbally and physically harassed at work. Women are often denied access to health services and supports, and face risks of violence when traveling between their home and workplace.

Intimate Partner Violence (IPV), perpetrated as a private or a family matter, is considered to be a serious problem in Thailand. The prevalence rate of IPV among women aged 15-49 years was estimated at 36.7% between 2012 and 2013 respectively (Thai Health Promotion Foundation). The lifetime prevalence of IPV among Thai women was reported as high as 48.6%. The National Statistical Office reported that 33.3% of married women aged 15-49 years had experienced physical violence from their husbands in the past year, and 20.7% had experienced sexual violence (National Statistical Office, 2010). The Ministry of Public Health (MoPH) found that, in 2015, more than 3,977 women and children received medical treatment for injuries resulting from domestic violence.

#### Proportion of married women 15-49 years old who experienced physical violence in the past year, 2009

![Graph](graph.png)

The Domestic Violence Act enables government officials to make available medical, social, and psychological assistance to survivors of domestic violence. The Thai government has supplemented this law with a national action plan on gender equality and the empowerment of women and their families in crisis. One stop crisis centers provide physical and psychological health services to victims of domestic violence. These centers also provide legal advice and counseling for victims of domestic violence, and the protection of the perpetrator. The Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development Learning Centers have been established to train government officials on gender equality issues. These centers emphasize that women of gender discrimination create legal remedies and compensation. However, there has been criticism under CEDAW for the lack of actions to protect the survivors of gender-based violence. There is also a lack of data collection on the failure of regular and adequate complaints.

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## IV. Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Women and Gender Equity

From the adoption of the Beijing Platform for Action in 1995, Thailand has made efforts to integrate the international principles protecting women and gender equity into national policies and laws. The National Economic and Social Development Plan (NESDP) from 2002-2006, 2007-2011, and 2012-2016, and the 30th and 35th National Economic and Social Development Plans (NESDP), have emphasized the Beijing Platform for Action (BPfA), the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), and from the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

Thailand has made significant efforts to integrate the international principles and instruments into the national policies and laws. The government has also established mechanisms to monitor and evaluate the implementation of these principles and instruments, including the National Committee on Women's Development and Gender Equity.

A Working Group for the Implementation of SDG 5 supports the implementation of the Goal on Gender Equality. The Ministry of Social Development and Human Security, the Association to Promote the Potential of Women with Disabilities, and the Foundation for Women have been active in the Working Group. The Working Group also includes representatives from the private sector, labor unions, and academic institutions.

The Strategy for Women's Development Plan (2017-2021) in Thailand made mechanisms for protecting women's development. It aligns with SDG 5 (Gender Equality). The Strategies for the View of "Promoting Gender Equity and Empowerment of Women" were designed to promote gender equality, including the protection of women's rights and the elimination of discrimination against women.

The government has also implemented the National Action Plan on Women, Children, and Senior Citizens' Development. This plan aims to protect and promote the development of women, children, and senior citizens. The Office of the National Economic and Social Development Council (NESDC) implements a gender equality policy framework for the promotion of women's rights.

In 2015, Thailand passed the first law promoting gender equality, namely the Gender Equality Act B.E. 2558 (2015), which aims to provide protection to people from gender discrimination. It is the third country in ASEAN to pass such a law. The Gender Equality Act B.E. 2558 (2015) recognizes the different challenges faced by people whose gender identity does not conform to societal norms.

*Report on the Implementation of the Beijing Platform for Action 1995-2000, United Nations Development Fund for Women (UNIFEM), June 2003.*

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

not match their sex assigned at birth.[^1] However, the Act has been criticized by the CEDAW Committee for failing to address the root causes of gender-based violence and sexual coercion.

The Act also fails to recognize that women’s bodies themselves have been historically appropriated as instruments of male entitlement. The Act does not address the structural inequalities that perpetuate gender-based violence, such as unequal access to resources, limited economic opportunities for women, and lack of control over their own reproductive lives. The Act also fails to acknowledge the impact of cultural norms and practices on gender equality.

The weak gender dimensions in the policy have been observed by the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) during its review of Thailand’s implementation of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The CEDAW Committee expressed particular concern for the situation of rural Thai women, who are disproportionately affected by poverty and limited economic opportunities. CEDAW notes that Thai women, in particular those living in rural areas, face significant challenges related to gender equality and sustainable development.

The lack of a comprehensive approach to gender equality and the absence of specific measures to address the root causes of gender-based violence have been identified as key gaps in the policy. The Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) has recommended that Thailand take steps to address these issues, including the development of a national action plan on gender equality and sustainable development.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Gender Analysis and Recommendation

This gender analysis acts as an entry point for addressing gender equality and women’s empowerment through 11 strategic approaches, i.e., with interventions targeted specifically at women and girls and it is expected that the project will contribute to the achievement of the following gender outcomes:

- Addressing gender inequalities to strengthen (time) savings and provide greater opportunities for all rural people
- Increasing women’s participation in decision-making processes related to agriculture and natural resource management, and strengthening the climate resilience of households and communities, in the areas directly and indirectly targeted by the project.

Over 50% of project beneficiaries are expected to be women, please see table below. The project should ensure that gender equality is fully captured in project implementation. These include disadvantages related to:

- Access to information about climate change and related impacts on water and agriculture, and
- Decision-making related water use/management and agriculture.
- Access to technology and related training to advance climate resilient agriculture.

### Thailand Creates a Supportive Mechanism

Thailand creates a supportive mechanism to promote female business start-ups for agricultural businesses. The mechanism includes an existing agricultural cooperative as a community-based organization (CBO) and a local government agency. It also creates jobs for local residents.

- **Project Title:** Women's Empowerment and Business Development
- **Implementing Agency:** Krediet Bank has prepared a gender bond. The bank will provide loans for female entrepreneurs.

- **Project Duration:** 5 years

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROJECT

### Climate Services for Agriculture (CS4A)

**Target:** 50% females (25% in leadership roles) among users of climate services.

- **Objective:** Enhance the use of climate information and services by smallholder farmers to adopt climate-smart options best suited to their needs.
- **Outcome:** Increased adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices by at least 10,000 smallholder farmers.
- **Gender "mainstream" - "Tailor":** Target 50% of agricultural extension services and climate information to women farmers.

### Outcome 2: Improved water management through strengthened infrastructure complemented by ESA measures for greater resilience to climate change impacts

#### Activity 1: Climate-informed water resource management (CWRM)

- **Indicator:** Availability and reliability of water supply.
- **Baseline:** Captured in the baseline assessment.
- **Target:** Disaggregated by sex and age, with a minimum of 50% women and youth.
- **Output:** Disaggregated by gender, age, and location.

#### Activity 2: Complementation of grey infrastructure with this measure and improved water use efficiency

- **Indicator:** Water use efficiency and reliability.
- **Baseline:** Captured in the baseline assessment.
- **Target:** Disaggregated by gender, age, and location.

## GREEN CITIES FUND PROGRAM

### Objective: Increase gender responsiveness to climate change adaptation and mitigation activities.

#### Target: Number of women participating in climate change adaptation and mitigation activities.

#### Target: 50% participation of women in water user associations.

### Outcome 3: Reduce vulnerability of agriculture households in drought and flood prone areas through strengthened extension support and local planning.

#### Activity 1: Augmentation of climate information to household agriculture farmers through extension services.

- **Indicator:** 50% extension workers supported by gender responsive methodology.
- **Target:** 100%.

**Training of trainers (ToT) for lead farmers in drought prone areas.**
- Training of extension workers in CSA.
- Transformation household agriculture farmers into lead farmers with knowledge on climate smart agriculture, water management, soil health, weather indices, and early warning systems.

**Training of extension workers in CSA.**

- **After start of implementation and thereafter:** Note: This would allow for initial training of "Lead Extension Workers" who would then train other extension workers. # Annex B – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

### Activity 3.1: Implementation of on-farm water management practices to improve drought and flood resilience, increase agricultural productivity and support sustainable agriculture

**Indicator:** Extension services provide training in water management practices.

- **Target:** 100% of farmers receive training.
- **Baseline:** 50% of farmers received training as of December 2020.
- **Timeline:** By 2023.
- **Responsible Agency:** Ministry of Agriculture (MoA).
- **Note:** This will support the integration of gender-responsive water management practices into climate-smart agriculture to strengthen resilience.

### Activity 3.2: Promotion and participation of women in training, as agents of change towards sustainable agricultural practices

**Indicator:** Women participate in training activities.

- **Target:** 50% of beneficiaries are women.
- **Baseline:** 30% of participants were women as of December 2020.
- **Timeline:** By 2024.
- **Responsible Agency:** Ministry of Agriculture (MoA).
- **Note:** This will support the integration of gender-responsive water management practices into climate-smart agriculture to strengthen resilience.

### Activity 3.3: Making available drought-tolerant training materials that map for all to use

**Indicator:** Training materials are made available.

- **Target:** 100% of farmers have access to training materials.
- **Baseline:** 60% of farmers had access as of December 2020.
- **Timeline:** By 2025.
- **Responsible Agency:** Ministry of Agriculture (MoA).
